INTRODUCTION 


On my joining the Ph.D. course, I was kindly invited by 
Mrs. Rhys Davids to take up the editing of the Petakopadesa 
as the subject of my thesis. Mrs. Rhys Davids was moreover 
kind enough to lend me the use of the necessary materials. 


These are the following:— 


Br. 
B2. T Pudet MSS. in Burmese characters. 
B3. J 


S.  —Paper MS. in Sinhalese characters. 


Called by Hardy a most obscure text and by Fuchs! as 
offering insurmountable difficulties, the MSS. are far from 
being satisfactory from the point of view of an editor. Of 
the MSS., however, B3. is by far the most reliable. B2. very 
closely follows B3. discrepancies being probably due to the 
scribe. Br. and S., on the other hand, differ a good deal from 
B2. and B3. both in arrangement? and in the readings as will 
be evident from a glance at the foot-notesš. Unfortunately 
Br. and S. are both full of corrupt readings, S. being hopelessly 
so. The MSS. instead of coming to the aid of one another 
on points of difficulty have only added to the con- 
fusion. Vainly the editor has searched one MS. after 
another for enlightenment; only on rare occasions have 


1. Specimen des Petakopadesa by Rudolf Fuchs, being the first 
Chapter of the Petakopadesa (Arivasaccapakāsana-pathamabhūmi). 
This was offered and was accepted as a thesis for Ph.D. degree of the 
University of Berlin, 4th August 1908. 

2. For differences in arrangement see pp. 174 and 237. 

3. The profusion in the differences of readings in the foot-notes 
has been due to the Sinhalese scribe's confusion witb Burmese letters. 


I am inclined to believe that S. owes its origin to Bl. or to some other 
Burmese MS. to which Bl. also owes its origin. 


ii 
streaks of light been thrown on the dark spots in the MSS. by 
these repeated investigations. In the circumstances, I am 


afraid that in the present endeavour many a mistake will 
be found. 


The Petakopadesa, i.e. ‘Instruction in the Pitaka ', has 
been ascribed to Mahākaccāyana who is also the author of the 
Netti-Pakarana and other books. Every chapter of the 
present work ends with affirming the authorship of Mahākac- 
cāyana who is said to be Jambuvanavāsin or Dweller of the 
Rose-apple Grove. The Gandha-Vamsa also refers to the 
Petaka as a work of Mahākaccāyana.* According to tradi- 
tion in Burma, this Mahākaccāyana is no one other than the 
famous disciple of the Buddha. Prof. Hardy, however, 
followed by Dr. Geiger, rejects this assertion as being wholly 
unwarranted, and is of opinion that the name might be a 
fictitious one, or that the real name of the author was Kac- 
cāyana which was subsequently changed into Mahākaccāyana. 
Hardy, moreover, is of opinion that our author is altogether 
different from the grammarian Kaccáyana.* 


Tradition, as we have seen, ascribes the work to Mahākac- 
cāyana and regards the Pe/aka as part of the Pāli canonical 
works. This, however, cannot be maintained. Nowhere 
indeed is it mentioned in the official or quasi-official lists of 


I. This is the rendering given in the P. T. S. Dictionary. There 
are, however, differences of opinion regarding the meaning of the 
title of our work. Neumann (trans. Majjhima-Nikāya, 1, xi) thinks it 
means 'Introduction to the study of Pitaka ', and in this he means 
Sutta-Pitaka. But Petaka is not solely occupied with Sutta-Pitaka ; 
in fact, it refers to all the Canonical works. Petaka has the seme mean- 
ing as Pitaka, hence upadesa may mean information in the Pitakas. Or 
again, Petaka —Sanskr. Paitaka, may mean information about the 
Pitakas. Or it may mean, if Petaka can be reccgnised in the Petaki 
of the Bharhut Inscriptions, instructions of one studyinz or versed in 
the Pitakas. A Commentary could explain this, but unfortunately 
none has been found. 


2. J.P.T.S. 1886, p. 59; 1896, 3. Geiger, Pali Literatur und 
p. 68. Sprache, p. 18. 
4. Hardy, Netti. Introduction XXXII. 


iii 

the Pitaka texts which are due to Buddhaghosa. The 
consensus of opinion is that the Petaka is a post-canonical 
work. But then what is its exact date? The earliest mention 
of the Petakopadesa is found in Buddhaghosa's Atthasālint! 
and Vtswddhi-Magga.” In both these works, Petaka is 
cited as one of the authorities. At a later date, mention of 
the Petakopadesa is found in the Netti Commentary? Netti- 
Pakaraņa, however, is older than the Petakopadesa. The 
latter presupposes the former as will be evidenced from 
the text (Soļasahārā Netti etc.) and also from various other 
quotations. The date of the Netti has as yet remained 
undetermined and so is that of the Petaka. All that can be 
said at present is that both these works were written sometime 
between the 5th century A.D. and the date of the later Pāli 
Canonical works. In the words of Prof. Hardy, both Netti 
and Petaka are still ' floating dateless on the ocean of Indian 
Literature’. A more definite date could, however, be given 
if our Petaka could be recognised in the Petaki (z.e., the 
person who knows the Petaka) of the Bharhut Inscriptions.‘ 
It would thus restrict the date to the second or the beginning 
of the first century B.C. 


I have made an attempt at tracing the very numerous 
quotations from the sacred texts which are interspersed in 
the Petaka but this has by no means been exhaustive. In 
the first chapter of the present work, I have incorporated the 
references mentioned by Dr. Fuchs. And Netti has been a 
very useful guide throughout. In this work there are copious 
quotations from the " Netti-Pakarana, some of the Chapters 
viz; Sāsanapatthāna, Haàravibhanga, and Hārasampāta, 
being common to both. The same line of thought, the same 


I. Atthasálini by Muller, (1887), — 3. Netti, 241.: ayam ca attho 
p. 165. Petakopadesena vibhāve- 
2. Visuddhi-Magga by Mrs. C. A. tabbo. 
F. Rhys Davids, Vol. I, 4. Z.D.M.G 40, 1886, p. 74. 


p. 141. 


iv 
outlook of the author can be observed in both the Pefaka 
and the Netti. 


On account of the unsatisfactory nature of the MSS. it has 
been very difficult for me to give as mature a version of the 
work as I had expected to. Hence, my endeavour has been 
to prepare the ground-work of this very obscure text.
